Basic Information Device Dependency:Device-independentWhite Point:Variable (typically D50 or D65)Gamut:UnlimitedCreated:1976 Components L* (Lightness)0% to 100%a* (Green-Red)-125 to 125b* (Blue-Yellow)-125 to 125 Key Properties Perceptual Uniformity:YesHue Linearity:NoLightness Separation:Yes Conversion Graph LabXYZLabLChLabXYZOKLabLabLChOKLChUsageCommon Applications: Color management, Image processing, Color difference calculationsIndustries: Printing, Textile, Paint manufacturing, Color researchDetailed GuideAttributesL*: Lightness (0 to 100)a*: Green-Red axis (negative green, positive red)b*: Blue-Yellow axis (negative blue, positive yellow)Historical ContextStandardized by the CIE in 1976, Lab was designed to be perceptually uniform—equal distances in the space should correspond to equal perceived color differences. It’s device-independent: Lab describes what a color looks like, not how a particular device produces it. For decades it’s been the workhorse of color management, ICC profiles, and industrial colorimetry.Key CharacteristicsPerceptual uniformity: the founding goal. Largely successful, though the blue-purple region exhibits more error than Ottosson’s OKLab.Device independence: Lab values are defined relative to a standard illuminant, not a particular display or printer.Separated lightness: L* is orthogonal to the chromatic axes, enabling brightness adjustment without hue shift.AdvantagesPerceptually uniform (the CIE 1976 definition)Device-independent—the standard for cross-media color communicationCovers all perceivable colors (gamut exceeds any physical display)Delta-E calculations (color difference metrics) are native to this spaceDisadvantagesComputationally heavier than RGB conversions (piecewise f(t) function)Not directly displayable—out-of-gamut Lab values must be mapped to sRGB or similarUnintuitive for manual color selection (what does a* = -40 look like?)White PointsD50: graphic arts, printing, ICC profile standard. Lab’s default illuminant.D65: average daylight, digital imaging. Used by sRGB and Display P3.Conversions between D50 and D65 use Bradford chromatic adaptation.Color ModelComponentsL* (Lightness):L∗=0 (black) to 100 (diffuse white)L^* = 0 \\text{ (black) to } 100 \\text{ (diffuse white)}L∗=0 (black) to 100 (diffuse white)a* (Green-Red axis):a∗<0 (green) to a∗>0 (red)a^* < 0 \\text{ (green) to } a^* > 0 \\text{ (red)}a∗<0 (green) to a∗>0 (red)b* (Blue-Yellow axis):b∗<0 (blue) to b∗>0 (yellow)b^* < 0 \\text{ (blue) to } b^* > 0 \\text{ (yellow)}b∗<0 (blue) to b∗>0 (yellow)RepresentationA three-dimensional Cartesian space. L* is the vertical axis; the a*/b* plane describes chromaticity at each lightness level. The cylindrical variant (LCh) converts a*,b* to polar coordinates—chroma and hue.Color ConversionsXYZ to LabNormalize XYZ by the reference white point, apply a piecewise function that approximates a cube root, then scale into L*a*b*:f(t)={t3t>ϵκ t+16116otherwisef(t) = \\begin{cases} \\sqrt[3]{t} & t > \\epsilon \\\\ \\frac{\\kappa\\, t + 16}{116} & \\text{otherwise} \\end{cases}f(t)={3t​116κt+16​​t>ϵotherwise​L∗=116 f ⁣(YYn)−16,a∗=500[f ⁣(XXn)−f ⁣(YYn)],b∗=200[f ⁣(YYn)−f ⁣(ZZn)]L^* = 116\\, f\\!\\left(\\frac{Y}{Y_n}\\right) - 16, \\quad a^* = 500\\left[f\\!\\left(\\frac{X}{X_n}\\right) - f\\!\\left(\\frac{Y}{Y_n}\\right)\\right], \\quad b^* = 200\\left[f\\!\\left(\\frac{Y}{Y_n}\\right) - f\\!\\left(\\frac{Z}{Z_n}\\right)\\right]L∗=116f(Yn​Y​)−16,a∗=500[f(Xn​X​)−f(Yn​Y​)],b∗=200[f(Yn​Y​)−f(Zn​Z​)]where ϵ=21624389\\epsilon = \\tfrac{216}{24389}ϵ=24389216​ and κ=2438927\\kappa = \\tfrac{24389}{27}κ=2724389​.Lab to XYZInvert the L*a*b* formulas—recover the normalized XYZ ratios, apply the inverse piecewise function, then scale by the white point:fy=L∗+16116,fx=a∗500+fy,fz=fy−b∗200f_y = \\frac{L^* + 16}{116}, \\quad f_x = \\frac{a^*}{500} + f_y, \\quad f_z = f_y - \\frac{b^*}{200}fy​=116L∗+16​,fx​=500a∗​+fy​,fz​=fy​−200b∗​X=Xn⋅f−1(fx),Y=Yn⋅f−1(fy),Z=Zn⋅f−1(fz)X = X_n \\cdot f^{-1}(f_x), \\quad Y = Y_n \\cdot f^{-1}(f_y), \\quad Z = Z_n \\cdot f^{-1}(f_z)X=Xn​⋅f−1(fx​),Y=Yn​⋅f−1(fy​),Z=Zn​⋅f−1(fz​)ApplicationsICC color management: the internal representation for color profiles and device-independent color communication.Delta-E calculations: industrial quality control (textiles, paint, manufacturing) relies on Lab-based color difference formulas.Image processing: operations like sharpening and blurring benefit from the perceptual uniformity.Print production: gamut mapping between CMYK and RGB passes through Lab.Scientific colorimetry: the standard coordinate system for color measurement and analysis.","w":514.8,"h":465.1,"attributedTo":"self"}]}
